People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I think it's... it's really the fear, more than anything. Like I mentioned before, when my wife tries to slip a little butter onto my vegetables or sets down a plate that's just too full, my chest gets tight. I just get so nervous that I'll start putting the weight back on and lose all that control I've worked so hard to keep. 

Speaking of vegetables, we used to have the most marvelous garden in the backyard. Tomatoes, cucumbers, zucchini—oh, the zucchini! It would grow so fast you could almost watch it get bigger. My wife used to make this wonderful zucchini bread. She’d bake loaves and loaves of it and give it away to the neighbors. 

Our next-door neighbor back then was Mr. Henderson. He was a retired train conductor. Used to wear his old cap when he mowed the lawn. He had this incredible model train set in his basement. Took up the whole room! He built little mountains out of papier-mâché and painted tiny little trees. He even had a little diner that lit up from the inside. 

He invited my grandson over to see it once when he was just a little tyke. The boy was absolutely mesmerized. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now kids just want to play those video games where everything is on a screen. It's a shame, really. Working with your hands, building something from scratch... it builds character. 

But... oh, my, I've gone off the rails again, haven't I? Just like Mr. Henderson's trains! I'm so sorry, doctor. What were you asking me? Oh, right, the fear of gaining weight. Yes, it's definitely the fear. Losing that control over my weight... it just makes me terribly nervous.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, my wife, she thinks I'm not eating enough. We've been married for fifty-two years this October, you know. We had our honeymoon up in Maine, just a beautiful time of year, the leaves were just starting to turn all those reds and oranges. But anyway, she fusses over me a lot lately. \n\nMost days I just have a cup of coffee and a piece of toast in the morning. I still drink that Folgers, the classic roast, always have. I used to drink it out of a metal thermos back when I worked at the plant, before they closed it down in the nineties. Then maybe I'll have a little soup or some vegetables later on in the day. We used to grow our own tomatoes, big beefsteak ones, but the deer keep getting into the yard now and eating everything down to the stems. It's a real shame, I put up a fence and everything, but they just jump right over it. \n\nBut really, big meals just feel completely unnecessary at my age. I don't see what all her fussing is about, to be honest with you. Have you ever tried keeping deer out of a garden? It's practically a full-time job.\n\nDoctor: How much do you currently weigh?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor, Dr. Henderson—he's been my doctor since old Dr. Miller retired back in, oh, must have been '08—he says the number on the scale has gotten too low. I think I'm around 115 pounds now. But honestly, being a bit lighter just makes it so much easier on my knees. I used to have terrible aches in my joints, especially when it rained. You know how the barometer drops and you can just feel it in your bones? My grandfather used to say he could predict a storm better than the weatherman just by how his knuckles felt. Anyway, being lighter makes it a breeze to get around the yard and keep up with my morning walks. I walk down by the old creek every day, right past where the Miller boys used to have that rope swing. They took it down years ago, liability I suppose, but the tree is still there. Beautiful old oak. So really, I feel fine at this weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating more than you currently are?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't feel the need to eat more. In fact, when my wife tries to put butter on things or serves me a full plate, I get, well, nervous. I worry I'll start putting weight back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. It's like when we bought our first car, a '68 Ford Galaxie. Beautiful car, sky blue. I kept that thing spotless, washed it every Sunday after church, wouldn't let the kids eat in it. You work hard to keep something nice, you know? You want to maintain it. And she just piles the potatoes on, and I think, I can't do that. I just can't. Speaking of potatoes, we used to grow those too, Idaho russets, but the soil here is just too clay-heavy. Have you ever tried amending clay soil? It takes years of adding compost, just back-breaking work. But no, I don't want to eat more. I like things just the way they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, underweight? I don't really see why everyone is making such a fuss about my weight, to be honest. I mean, older folks are supposed to eat less, aren't they? My metabolism isn't what it used to be. And I'm still managing my chores just fine. I do my tours around the yard, check the fence line for where those deer might be squeezing through. You know, I found a spot near the old lilac bush where the wire was bent down. I think it might be raccoons, actually. We had a raccoon problem back in '92, got right into the garbage cans every Tuesday night without fail. Smart creatures, they figure out the latches. I had to bungee cord the lids down. But anyway, I'm active, I'm moving around, I feel light on my feet. So no, I don't see the problem. I'm doing just fine. Did I mention I fixed that fence myself? Just needed some heavy-duty pliers and a bit of elbow grease.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose \"afraid\" is one way to put it. Like I was telling you a minute ago about my wife and her cooking... when she tries to sneak butter onto my vegetables or hands me one of those overflowing plates, I just get so nervous. I really do. I worry I'll start putting all that weight right back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nIt's all about keeping things in order, you see. I've always been a man who likes order. You should see my workbench out in the garage. Every wrench, every single screwdriver has its own little outline drawn on the pegboard. I traced them all out back in, oh, must have been the summer of '85. Used a thick red marker. You can't even buy markers of that quality anymore, the ones they sell at the store today dry out if you leave the cap off for five minutes. We used to get all our tools and supplies down at Miller's Hardware—no relation to the old doctor I mentioned—and they had these big wooden barrels where you could just weigh out your own nails by the pound. The whole place smelled like linseed oil and cut pine. It was a wonderful smell, they just don't have stores like that anymore. \n\nBut yes, the thought of gaining it back... it just makes my stomach tie up in knots, so I have to keep a tight rein on things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin?\n\nPatient: Too thin? Well, I don't know about too thin. I look in the mirror and... well, it's about standards, really. If my belt feels a notch looser, I feel like I've done right by myself. It's a good feeling. But if my stomach looks the least bit rounded in the mirror, I just feel sloppy and disappointed. It's like letting yourself go. \n\nSpeaking of belts, I've had this one leather belt for... oh, it has to be thirty years now. Genuine cowhide, made in Texas. You just can't find leather like that anymore. Everything now is that bonded stuff that peels apart after a year. I used to polish it every month with saddle soap, the same tin of saddle soap I bought when the kids were still in grade school. The tin has a little dent on the side from when my youngest, Tommy, knocked it off the porch railing trying to catch a firefly. We used to have jars and jars of fireflies on the porch in the summer. You don't see as many fireflies these days, do you? I read somewhere it's because of all the streetlights confusing them. Or maybe it's the pesticides. We never used pesticides on our lawn, just let the clover grow. Clover is good for the bees, you know. But anyway, no, I don't think I look too thin. I just don't want to look sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about how your body looks if you gain weight?\n\nPatient: Worry about how it looks? Well, I don't know about that. I'm not exactly trying to be a movie star at my age. I don't stand there fretting over my reflection, if that's what you mean. \n\nSpeaking of movie stars, though, did you ever go to the old Majestic Theater downtown before they tore it down? Oh, it was a beautiful building. They had real brass fixtures out in the lobby and these heavy, dark red velvet curtains that would pull back right before the picture started. My wife and I, our very first date was at the Majestic. We saw a double feature, must have been 1964 or '65. Popcorn was practically pennies back then. Now you go to a theater and you have to take out a second mortgage just to get a soda. And the floors are always sticky now, have you noticed that? \n\nAt the Majestic, they had an usher with a little flashlight who would actually sweep up between the showings. A young fellow named Arthur, he lived just down the street from my parents. He kept that place spotless. Joined the Navy later on, I believe, and moved out to California. But anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I don't really sit around worrying about my figure or anything like that. I just like to keep things exactly the way they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight affects how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's exactly what brings me in here today. My wife is convinced I'm not eating enough, always fussing over my plate and watching me. But really, most days I just have a cup of coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and then maybe some soup or a few vegetables later on. Big meals just feel completely unnecessary at my age. You just don't need all that heavy food when you're getting on in years. \n\nAnd speaking of coffee, I still make mine in one of those old stovetop percolators. You know the ones, with the little glass knob on the lid? You just watch the dark water bubble up into the glass. My mother had one exactly like it when I was a boy. She kept it on the back burner from dawn till dusk. We used to get our coffee beans from a little grocer down on 4th street, a fellow named Mr. Henderson. He had this massive cast-iron grinder right there on the checkout counter. It made a terrible racket, just a deafening grinding noise, but oh, the smell was incredible. \n\nMr. Henderson had a glass eye, you know. Lost his real one in the First World War, over in France somewhere. He used to tell us kids he could see right through the glass one if he squinted hard enough, and we believed him! We really did. Kids will believe just about anything you tell them, won't they? We used to stand there staring at his eye while he ground the coffee, wondering what he was looking at. \n\nBut... oh, what did you ask me? I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Right, about my eating. Yes, just coffee and toast for me, maybe some soup. Like I said, my wife worries, but I just don't see the need for big meals anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat less because you want to maintain a certain shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I did just mention that a minute ago, didn't I? It's like I was saying, if my belt feels a notch looser, I feel like I've done right by myself. It's a good feeling, you know? But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit rounded, I just feel sloppy. Disappointed in myself. I don't want to look like I've let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of letting things go, you should see the state of the old community center down the road from us. It's an absolute shame. The paint is peeling off in sheets, and half the windows are boarded up. We used to have our town hall meetings there, and the local rotary club would host a pancake breakfast every spring. My wife and I would volunteer to flip the pancakes. We had these huge griddles, must have been three feet across. \n\nOne year, the mayor—Mayor Wilkins, this was back in the late seventies—he tried to flip a pancake and it landed right on his shoe! A brand new pair of wingtips, ruined with syrup. He was a good sport about it, though. Laughed it off. You don't see politicians like that anymore, do you? Now they're all so stiff and worried about their image on the television. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Right, my appearance. Yes, I just don't want to look sloppy. That's why I like my belt to feel a bit loose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating more would make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Well, yes, I suppose I do. When my wife tries to put butter on things or, you know, serves me a full plate of dinner, I get nervous. I just worry that I'll start putting the weight back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of butter, did you know they used to deliver it right to your door? Oh yes, milk and butter, right in a little insulated box on the porch. The milkman we had when we first bought our house, his name was Charlie. He drove one of those funny little trucks where you stand up to drive. He always left a little treat for our dog, Buster. Buster was a golden retriever mix, sweetest dog you'd ever meet, but he had a terrible habit of chasing the mailman. Never the milkman, though! Charlie had him bought and paid for with those dog biscuits. \n\nCharlie's route got bought out by one of the big dairies eventually, and they stopped doing home delivery altogether. It's a shame how everything just gets swallowed up by the big corporations nowadays, isn't it? You can't even find a decent local hardware store anymore, just those massive warehouses where nobody knows your name and you can never find anyone to help you find a simple wood screw. \n\nBut... oh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I apologize. What was the question? Ah, yes, the anxiety. Yes, the full plates and the butter, it just makes me nervous about losing my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to maintain a certain weight is more about how you feel or how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's a bit of both, really. Like I was saying before, it's about that feeling of doing right by myself when my belt is a notch looser. But it's also how I look in the mirror—if I see even the slightest curve to my stomach, I just feel so sloppy. It's disappointing. \n\nYou know, speaking of mirrors, we used to have this magnificent antique mirror in our front hallway. It had this heavy, carved wooden frame, mahogany, I think. My wife's grandfather brought it over from Ireland when he immigrated. They came over on a boat, took weeks to cross the Atlantic. Can you imagine? Being stuck on a ship that long, with just a trunk of belongings? \n\nHe settled in Boston first, worked on the docks for a while. It was tough work, hauling crates all day in the freezing cold. He used to tell stories about how the harbor would freeze over in the winter, and they'd have to break the ice just to get the ships in. People were tougher back then, I think. They had to be. Nowadays, people complain if the Wi-Fi goes out for five minutes. \n\nMy grandson, he's always on that phone of his. I ask him what he's looking at, and he says he's talking to his friends. But they're not even talking! Just typing with their thumbs. It's a strange world we live in now. \n\nBut... oh, goodness, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry, doctor. What were we discussing? Ah, the weight and how I look. Yes, it's just that I don't want to look sloppy. That loose belt just makes me feel better about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more driven by a fear of gaining weight or by how you want to look?\n\nPatient: Well, I think it's... it's really the fear, more than anything. Like I mentioned before, when my wife tries to slip a little butter onto my vegetables or sets down a plate that's just too full, my chest gets tight. I just get so nervous that I'll start putting the weight back on and lose all that control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of vegetables, we used to have the most marvelous garden in the backyard. Tomatoes, cucumbers, zucchini—oh, the zucchini! It would grow so fast you could almost watch it get bigger. My wife used to make this wonderful zucchini bread. She’d bake loaves and loaves of it and give it away to the neighbors. \n\nOur next-door neighbor back then was Mr. Henderson. He was a retired train conductor. Used to wear his old cap when he mowed the lawn. He had this incredible model train set in his basement. Took up the whole room! He built little mountains out of papier-mâché and painted tiny little trees. He even had a little diner that lit up from the inside. \n\nHe invited my grandson over to see it once when he was just a little tyke. The boy was absolutely mesmerized. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now kids just want to play those video games where everything is on a screen. It's a shame, really. Working with your hands, building something from scratch... it builds character. \n\nBut... oh, my, I've gone off the rails again, haven't I? Just like Mr. Henderson's trains! I'm so sorry, doctor. What were you asking me? Oh, right, the fear of gaining weight. Yes, it's definitely the fear. Losing that control over my weight... it just makes me terribly nervous.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It made a terrible racket, just a deafening grinding noise, but oh, the smell was incredible. \n\nMr. Henderson had a glass eye, you know. Lost his real one in the First World War, over in France somewhere. He used to tell us kids he could see right through the glass one if he squinted hard enough, and we believed him! We really did. Kids will believe just about anything you tell them, won't they? We used to stand there staring at his eye while he ground the coffee, wondering what he was looking at. \n\nBut... oh, what did you ask me? I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Right, about my eating. Yes, just coffee and toast for me, maybe some soup. Like I said, my wife worries, but I just don't see the need for big meals anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat less because you want to maintain a certain shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I did just mention that a minute ago, didn't I? It's like I was saying, if my belt feels a notch looser, I feel like I've done right by myself. It's a good feeling, you know? But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit rounded, I just feel sloppy. Disappointed in myself. I don't want to look like I've let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of letting things go, you should see the state of the old community center down the road from us. It's an absolute shame. The paint is peeling off in sheets, and half the windows are boarded up. We used to have our town hall meetings there, and the local rotary club would host a pancake breakfast every spring. My wife and I would volunteer to flip the pancakes. We had these huge griddles, must have been three feet across. \n\nOne year, the mayor—Mayor Wilkins, this was back in the late seventies—he tried to flip a pancake and it landed right on his shoe! A brand new pair of wingtips, ruined with syrup. He was a good sport about it, though. Laughed it off. You don't see politicians like that anymore, do you? Now they're all so stiff and worried about their image on the television. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Right, my appearance. Yes, I just don't want to look sloppy. That's why I like my belt to feel a bit loose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating more would make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Well, yes, I suppose I do. When my wife tries to put butter on things or, you know, serves me a full plate of dinner, I get nervous. I just worry that I'll start putting the weight back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of butter, did you know they used to deliver it right to your door? Oh yes, milk and butter, right in a little insulated box on the porch. The milkman we had when we first bought our house, his name was Charlie. He drove one of those funny little trucks where you stand up to drive. He always left a little treat for our dog, Buster. Buster was a golden retriever mix, sweetest dog you'd ever meet, but he had a terrible habit of chasing the mailman. Never the milkman, though! Charlie had him bought and paid for with those dog biscuits. \n\nCharlie's route got bought out by one of the big dairies eventually, and they stopped doing home delivery altogether. It's a shame how everything just gets swallowed up by the big corporations nowadays, isn't it? You can't even find a decent local hardware store anymore, just those massive warehouses where nobody knows your name and you can never find anyone to help you find a simple wood screw. \n\nBut... oh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I apologize. What was the question? Ah, yes, the anxiety. Yes, the full plates and the butter, it just makes me nervous about losing my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to maintain a certain weight is more about how you feel or how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's a bit of both, really. Like I was saying before, it's about that feeling of doing right by myself when my belt is a notch looser. But it's also how I look in the mirror—if I see even the slightest curve to my stomach, I just feel so sloppy. It's disappointing. \n\nYou know, speaking of mirrors, we used to have this magnificent antique mirror in our front hallway. It had this heavy, carved wooden frame, mahogany, I think. My wife's grandfather brought it over from Ireland when he immigrated. They came over on a boat, took weeks to cross the Atlantic. Can you imagine? Being stuck on a ship that long, with just a trunk of belongings? \n\nHe settled in Boston first, worked on the docks for a while. It was tough work, hauling crates all day in the freezing cold. He used to tell stories about how the harbor would freeze over in the winter, and they'd have to break the ice just to get the ships in. People were tougher back then, I think. They had to be. Nowadays, people complain if the Wi-Fi goes out for five minutes. \n\nMy grandson, he's always on that phone of his. I ask him what he's looking at, and he says he's talking to his friends. But they're not even talking! Just typing with their thumbs. It's a strange world we live in now. \n\nBut... oh, goodness, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry, doctor. What were we discussing? Ah, the weight and how I look. Yes, it's just that I don't want to look sloppy. That loose belt just makes me feel better about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more driven by a fear of gaining weight or by how you want to look?\n\nPatient: Well, I think it's... it's really the fear, more than anything. Like I mentioned before, when my wife tries to slip a little butter onto my vegetables or sets down a plate that's just too full, my chest gets tight. I just get so nervous that I'll start putting the weight back on and lose all that control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of vegetables, we used to have the most marvelous garden in the backyard. Tomatoes, cucumbers, zucchini—oh, the zucchini! It would grow so fast you could almost watch it get bigger. My wife used to make this wonderful zucchini bread. She’d bake loaves and loaves of it and give it away to the neighbors. \n\nOur next-door neighbor back then was Mr. Henderson. He was a retired train conductor. Used to wear his old cap when he mowed the lawn. He had this incredible model train set in his basement. Took up the whole room! He built little mountains out of papier-mâché and painted tiny little trees. He even had a little diner that lit up from the inside. \n\nHe invited my grandson over to see it once when he was just a little tyke. The boy was absolutely mesmerized. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now kids just want to play those video games where everything is on a screen. It's a shame, really. Working with your hands, building something from scratch... it builds character. \n\nBut... oh, my, I've gone off the rails again, haven't I? Just like Mr. Henderson's trains! I'm so sorry, doctor. What were you asking me? Oh, right, the fear of gaining weight. Yes, it's definitely the fear. Losing that control over my weight... it just makes me terribly nervous.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Well, I suppose the fear plays a part, but honestly, what really brought me in today is that my wife thinks I'm not eating enough. I try to tell her, most days I just have my coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and that's plenty. 

Speaking of coffee, we used to have this old aluminum percolator that we kept on the stove. Made the absolute best coffee, strong enough to wake the dead. My father used to drink it black as pitch. He worked at the old auto plant, you see. Thirty-two years on the assembly line, putting on car doors. He’d be up at four in the morning every single day, never even needed an alarm clock. He just had this beautiful silver pocket watch. I still have it, actually, sitting in my top dresser drawer. You have to wind it by hand every day or it stops ticking. They really built things to last back then, didn't they? Now you buy a toaster and the heating element burns out in six months. It’s all just cheap plastic and wires. 

But... oh, listen to me rambling on about toasters. I apologize, doctor. What were we saying? Oh, right, my meals. Yes, so just the coffee and toast, and then maybe some soup or a few vegetables later in the day. At my age, big meals just feel completely unnecessary, you know?

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, my wife, she thinks I'm not eating enough. We've been married for fifty-two years this October, you know. We had our honeymoon up in Maine, just a beautiful time of year, the leaves were just starting to turn all those reds and oranges. But anyway, she fusses over me a lot lately. \n\nMost days I just have a cup of coffee and a piece of toast in the morning. I still drink that Folgers, the classic roast, always have. I used to drink it out of a metal thermos back when I worked at the plant, before they closed it down in the nineties. Then maybe I'll have a little soup or some vegetables later on in the day. We used to grow our own tomatoes, big beefsteak ones, but the deer keep getting into the yard now and eating everything down to the stems. It's a real shame, I put up a fence and everything, but they just jump right over it. \n\nBut really, big meals just feel completely unnecessary at my age. I don't see what all her fussing is about, to be honest with you. Have you ever tried keeping deer out of a garden? It's practically a full-time job.\n\nDoctor: How much do you currently weigh?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor, Dr. Henderson—he's been my doctor since old Dr. Miller retired back in, oh, must have been '08—he says the number on the scale has gotten too low. I think I'm around 115 pounds now. But honestly, being a bit lighter just makes it so much easier on my knees. I used to have terrible aches in my joints, especially when it rained. You know how the barometer drops and you can just feel it in your bones? My grandfather used to say he could predict a storm better than the weatherman just by how his knuckles felt. Anyway, being lighter makes it a breeze to get around the yard and keep up with my morning walks. I walk down by the old creek every day, right past where the Miller boys used to have that rope swing. They took it down years ago, liability I suppose, but the tree is still there. Beautiful old oak. So really, I feel fine at this weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating more than you currently are?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't feel the need to eat more. In fact, when my wife tries to put butter on things or serves me a full plate, I get, well, nervous. I worry I'll start putting weight back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. It's like when we bought our first car, a '68 Ford Galaxie. Beautiful car, sky blue. I kept that thing spotless, washed it every Sunday after church, wouldn't let the kids eat in it. You work hard to keep something nice, you know? You want to maintain it. And she just piles the potatoes on, and I think, I can't do that. I just can't. Speaking of potatoes, we used to grow those too, Idaho russets, but the soil here is just too clay-heavy. Have you ever tried amending clay soil? It takes years of adding compost, just back-breaking work. But no, I don't want to eat more. I like things just the way they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, underweight? I don't really see why everyone is making such a fuss about my weight, to be honest. I mean, older folks are supposed to eat less, aren't they? My metabolism isn't what it used to be. And I'm still managing my chores just fine. I do my tours around the yard, check the fence line for where those deer might be squeezing through. You know, I found a spot near the old lilac bush where the wire was bent down. I think it might be raccoons, actually. We had a raccoon problem back in '92, got right into the garbage cans every Tuesday night without fail. Smart creatures, they figure out the latches. I had to bungee cord the lids down. But anyway, I'm active, I'm moving around, I feel light on my feet. So no, I don't see the problem. I'm doing just fine. Did I mention I fixed that fence myself? Just needed some heavy-duty pliers and a bit of elbow grease.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose \"afraid\" is one way to put it. Like I was telling you a minute ago about my wife and her cooking... when she tries to sneak butter onto my vegetables or hands me one of those overflowing plates, I just get so nervous. I really do. I worry I'll start putting all that weight right back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nIt's all about keeping things in order, you see. I've always been a man who likes order. You should see my workbench out in the garage. Every wrench, every single screwdriver has its own little outline drawn on the pegboard. I traced them all out back in, oh, must have been the summer of '85. Used a thick red marker. You can't even buy markers of that quality anymore, the ones they sell at the store today dry out if you leave the cap off for five minutes. We used to get all our tools and supplies down at Miller's Hardware—no relation to the old doctor I mentioned—and they had these big wooden barrels where you could just weigh out your own nails by the pound. The whole place smelled like linseed oil and cut pine. It was a wonderful smell, they just don't have stores like that anymore. \n\nBut yes, the thought of gaining it back... it just makes my stomach tie up in knots, so I have to keep a tight rein on things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin?\n\nPatient: Too thin? Well, I don't know about too thin. I look in the mirror and... well, it's about standards, really. If my belt feels a notch looser, I feel like I've done right by myself. It's a good feeling. But if my stomach looks the least bit rounded in the mirror, I just feel sloppy and disappointed. It's like letting yourself go. \n\nSpeaking of belts, I've had this one leather belt for... oh, it has to be thirty years now. Genuine cowhide, made in Texas. You just can't find leather like that anymore. Everything now is that bonded stuff that peels apart after a year. I used to polish it every month with saddle soap, the same tin of saddle soap I bought when the kids were still in grade school. The tin has a little dent on the side from when my youngest, Tommy, knocked it off the porch railing trying to catch a firefly. We used to have jars and jars of fireflies on the porch in the summer. You don't see as many fireflies these days, do you? I read somewhere it's because of all the streetlights confusing them. Or maybe it's the pesticides. We never used pesticides on our lawn, just let the clover grow. Clover is good for the bees, you know. But anyway, no, I don't think I look too thin. I just don't want to look sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about how your body looks if you gain weight?\n\nPatient: Worry about how it looks? Well, I don't know about that. I'm not exactly trying to be a movie star at my age. I don't stand there fretting over my reflection, if that's what you mean. \n\nSpeaking of movie stars, though, did you ever go to the old Majestic Theater downtown before they tore it down? Oh, it was a beautiful building. They had real brass fixtures out in the lobby and these heavy, dark red velvet curtains that would pull back right before the picture started. My wife and I, our very first date was at the Majestic. We saw a double feature, must have been 1964 or '65. Popcorn was practically pennies back then. Now you go to a theater and you have to take out a second mortgage just to get a soda. And the floors are always sticky now, have you noticed that? \n\nAt the Majestic, they had an usher with a little flashlight who would actually sweep up between the showings. A young fellow named Arthur, he lived just down the street from my parents. He kept that place spotless. Joined the Navy later on, I believe, and moved out to California. But anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I don't really sit around worrying about my figure or anything like that. I just like to keep things exactly the way they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight affects how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's exactly what brings me in here today. My wife is convinced I'm not eating enough, always fussing over my plate and watching me. But really, most days I just have a cup of coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and then maybe some soup or a few vegetables later on. Big meals just feel completely unnecessary at my age. You just don't need all that heavy food when you're getting on in years. \n\nAnd speaking of coffee, I still make mine in one of those old stovetop percolators. You know the ones, with the little glass knob on the lid? You just watch the dark water bubble up into the glass. My mother had one exactly like it when I was a boy. She kept it on the back burner from dawn till dusk. We used to get our coffee beans from a little grocer down on 4th street, a fellow named Mr. Henderson. He had this massive cast-iron grinder right there on the checkout counter. It made a terrible racket, just a deafening grinding noise, but oh, the smell was incredible. \n\nMr. Henderson had a glass eye, you know. Lost his real one in the First World War, over in France somewhere. He used to tell us kids he could see right through the glass one if he squinted hard enough, and we believed him! We really did. Kids will believe just about anything you tell them, won't they? We used to stand there staring at his eye while he ground the coffee, wondering what he was looking at. \n\nBut... oh, what did you ask me? I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Right, about my eating. Yes, just coffee and toast for me, maybe some soup. Like I said, my wife worries, but I just don't see the need for big meals anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat less because you want to maintain a certain shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I did just mention that a minute ago, didn't I? It's like I was saying, if my belt feels a notch looser, I feel like I've done right by myself. It's a good feeling, you know? But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit rounded, I just feel sloppy. Disappointed in myself. I don't want to look like I've let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of letting things go, you should see the state of the old community center down the road from us. It's an absolute shame. The paint is peeling off in sheets, and half the windows are boarded up. We used to have our town hall meetings there, and the local rotary club would host a pancake breakfast every spring. My wife and I would volunteer to flip the pancakes. We had these huge griddles, must have been three feet across. \n\nOne year, the mayor—Mayor Wilkins, this was back in the late seventies—he tried to flip a pancake and it landed right on his shoe! A brand new pair of wingtips, ruined with syrup. He was a good sport about it, though. Laughed it off. You don't see politicians like that anymore, do you? Now they're all so stiff and worried about their image on the television. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Right, my appearance. Yes, I just don't want to look sloppy. That's why I like my belt to feel a bit loose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating more would make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Well, yes, I suppose I do. When my wife tries to put butter on things or, you know, serves me a full plate of dinner, I get nervous. I just worry that I'll start putting the weight back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of butter, did you know they used to deliver it right to your door? Oh yes, milk and butter, right in a little insulated box on the porch. The milkman we had when we first bought our house, his name was Charlie. He drove one of those funny little trucks where you stand up to drive. He always left a little treat for our dog, Buster. Buster was a golden retriever mix, sweetest dog you'd ever meet, but he had a terrible habit of chasing the mailman. Never the milkman, though! Charlie had him bought and paid for with those dog biscuits. \n\nCharlie's route got bought out by one of the big dairies eventually, and they stopped doing home delivery altogether. It's a shame how everything just gets swallowed up by the big corporations nowadays, isn't it? You can't even find a decent local hardware store anymore, just those massive warehouses where nobody knows your name and you can never find anyone to help you find a simple wood screw. \n\nBut... oh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I apologize. What was the question? Ah, yes, the anxiety. Yes, the full plates and the butter, it just makes me nervous about losing my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to maintain a certain weight is more about how you feel or how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's a bit of both, really. Like I was saying before, it's about that feeling of doing right by myself when my belt is a notch looser. But it's also how I look in the mirror—if I see even the slightest curve to my stomach, I just feel so sloppy. It's disappointing. \n\nYou know, speaking of mirrors, we used to have this magnificent antique mirror in our front hallway. It had this heavy, carved wooden frame, mahogany, I think. My wife's grandfather brought it over from Ireland when he immigrated. They came over on a boat, took weeks to cross the Atlantic. Can you imagine? Being stuck on a ship that long, with just a trunk of belongings? \n\nHe settled in Boston first, worked on the docks for a while. It was tough work, hauling crates all day in the freezing cold. He used to tell stories about how the harbor would freeze over in the winter, and they'd have to break the ice just to get the ships in. People were tougher back then, I think. They had to be. Nowadays, people complain if the Wi-Fi goes out for five minutes. \n\nMy grandson, he's always on that phone of his. I ask him what he's looking at, and he says he's talking to his friends. But they're not even talking! Just typing with their thumbs. It's a strange world we live in now. \n\nBut... oh, goodness, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry, doctor. What were we discussing? Ah, the weight and how I look. Yes, it's just that I don't want to look sloppy. That loose belt just makes me feel better about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more driven by a fear of gaining weight or by how you want to look?\n\nPatient: Well, I think it's... it's really the fear, more than anything. Like I mentioned before, when my wife tries to slip a little butter onto my vegetables or sets down a plate that's just too full, my chest gets tight. I just get so nervous that I'll start putting the weight back on and lose all that control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of vegetables, we used to have the most marvelous garden in the backyard. Tomatoes, cucumbers, zucchini—oh, the zucchini! It would grow so fast you could almost watch it get bigger. My wife used to make this wonderful zucchini bread. She’d bake loaves and loaves of it and give it away to the neighbors. \n\nOur next-door neighbor back then was Mr. Henderson. He was a retired train conductor. Used to wear his old cap when he mowed the lawn. He had this incredible model train set in his basement. Took up the whole room! He built little mountains out of papier-mâché and painted tiny little trees. He even had a little diner that lit up from the inside. \n\nHe invited my grandson over to see it once when he was just a little tyke. The boy was absolutely mesmerized. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now kids just want to play those video games where everything is on a screen. It's a shame, really. Working with your hands, building something from scratch... it builds character. \n\nBut... oh, my, I've gone off the rails again, haven't I? Just like Mr. Henderson's trains! I'm so sorry, doctor. What were you asking me? Oh, right, the fear of gaining weight. Yes, it's definitely the fear. Losing that control over my weight... it just makes me terribly nervous.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the fear plays a part, but honestly, what really brought me in today is that my wife thinks I'm not eating enough. I try to tell her, most days I just have my coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and that's plenty. \n\nSpeaking of coffee, we used to have this old aluminum percolator that we kept on the stove. Made the absolute best coffee, strong enough to wake the dead. My father used to drink it black as pitch. He worked at the old auto plant, you see. Thirty-two years on the assembly line, putting on car doors. He’d be up at four in the morning every single day, never even needed an alarm clock. He just had this beautiful silver pocket watch. I still have it, actually, sitting in my top dresser drawer. You have to wind it by hand every day or it stops ticking. They really built things to last back then, didn't they? Now you buy a toaster and the heating element burns out in six months. It’s all just cheap plastic and wires. \n\nBut... oh, listen to me rambling on about toasters. I apologize, doctor. What were we saying? Oh, right, my meals. Yes, so just the coffee and toast, and then maybe some soup or a few vegetables later in the day. At my age, big meals just feel completely unnecessary, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose it really comes down to the fear of putting the weight back on. I mean, I think I mentioned this just a bit ago, but if my wife tries to slip a little butter onto my vegetables, or if she sets down a plate in front of me that just looks too full... my chest gets tight. I just get so nervous that I'll start gaining it all back and lose all that control I've worked so hard to keep. 

Speaking of control, you know, my father used to drive this old Ford pickup truck. Manual transmission, column shift—three on the tree, they called it. You really had to know how to handle a clutch back then. He taught me how to drive it out in the empty parking lot behind the old textile mill on Sundays. That mill employed half the town back in the fifties. They made these thick woolen blankets. Every house had one on the bed in the winter. Heavy things, weighed about ten pounds, but they kept you warm even when the furnace couldn't keep up. 

The mill shut down, oh, must have been in the late seventies. Sent all the jobs overseas. It was a real blow to the community. People had to pack up and move away just to find work. The old building sat empty for decades, just crumbling away. Kids used to sneak in there and break the windows. Just a few years ago, some developer bought the land and knocked the whole thing down to build luxury condominiums. Can you imagine? Condominiums in our little town. They painted them all these drab gray colors. No character at all. 

But... oh, goodness, I'm wandering off again, aren't I? I get to talking about the old days and I just lose my train of thought. I'm sorry, doctor. You were asking about why I limit my food. Yes, it's the fear of gaining the weight back. I just can't stand the thought of losing that control.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello doctor. I suppose what brings me in is... well, my wife, she thinks I'm not eating enough. We've been married for fifty-two years this October, you know. We had our honeymoon up in Maine, just a beautiful time of year, the leaves were just starting to turn all those reds and oranges. But anyway, she fusses over me a lot lately. \n\nMost days I just have a cup of coffee and a piece of toast in the morning. I still drink that Folgers, the classic roast, always have. I used to drink it out of a metal thermos back when I worked at the plant, before they closed it down in the nineties. Then maybe I'll have a little soup or some vegetables later on in the day. We used to grow our own tomatoes, big beefsteak ones, but the deer keep getting into the yard now and eating everything down to the stems. It's a real shame, I put up a fence and everything, but they just jump right over it. \n\nBut really, big meals just feel completely unnecessary at my age. I don't see what all her fussing is about, to be honest with you. Have you ever tried keeping deer out of a garden? It's practically a full-time job.\n\nDoctor: How much do you currently weigh?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor, Dr. Henderson—he's been my doctor since old Dr. Miller retired back in, oh, must have been '08—he says the number on the scale has gotten too low. I think I'm around 115 pounds now. But honestly, being a bit lighter just makes it so much easier on my knees. I used to have terrible aches in my joints, especially when it rained. You know how the barometer drops and you can just feel it in your bones? My grandfather used to say he could predict a storm better than the weatherman just by how his knuckles felt. Anyway, being lighter makes it a breeze to get around the yard and keep up with my morning walks. I walk down by the old creek every day, right past where the Miller boys used to have that rope swing. They took it down years ago, liability I suppose, but the tree is still there. Beautiful old oak. So really, I feel fine at this weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating more than you currently are?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't feel the need to eat more. In fact, when my wife tries to put butter on things or serves me a full plate, I get, well, nervous. I worry I'll start putting weight back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. It's like when we bought our first car, a '68 Ford Galaxie. Beautiful car, sky blue. I kept that thing spotless, washed it every Sunday after church, wouldn't let the kids eat in it. You work hard to keep something nice, you know? You want to maintain it. And she just piles the potatoes on, and I think, I can't do that. I just can't. Speaking of potatoes, we used to grow those too, Idaho russets, but the soil here is just too clay-heavy. Have you ever tried amending clay soil? It takes years of adding compost, just back-breaking work. But no, I don't want to eat more. I like things just the way they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, underweight? I don't really see why everyone is making such a fuss about my weight, to be honest. I mean, older folks are supposed to eat less, aren't they? My metabolism isn't what it used to be. And I'm still managing my chores just fine. I do my tours around the yard, check the fence line for where those deer might be squeezing through. You know, I found a spot near the old lilac bush where the wire was bent down. I think it might be raccoons, actually. We had a raccoon problem back in '92, got right into the garbage cans every Tuesday night without fail. Smart creatures, they figure out the latches. I had to bungee cord the lids down. But anyway, I'm active, I'm moving around, I feel light on my feet. So no, I don't see the problem. I'm doing just fine. Did I mention I fixed that fence myself? Just needed some heavy-duty pliers and a bit of elbow grease.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose \"afraid\" is one way to put it. Like I was telling you a minute ago about my wife and her cooking... when she tries to sneak butter onto my vegetables or hands me one of those overflowing plates, I just get so nervous. I really do. I worry I'll start putting all that weight right back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nIt's all about keeping things in order, you see. I've always been a man who likes order. You should see my workbench out in the garage. Every wrench, every single screwdriver has its own little outline drawn on the pegboard. I traced them all out back in, oh, must have been the summer of '85. Used a thick red marker. You can't even buy markers of that quality anymore, the ones they sell at the store today dry out if you leave the cap off for five minutes. We used to get all our tools and supplies down at Miller's Hardware—no relation to the old doctor I mentioned—and they had these big wooden barrels where you could just weigh out your own nails by the pound. The whole place smelled like linseed oil and cut pine. It was a wonderful smell, they just don't have stores like that anymore. \n\nBut yes, the thought of gaining it back... it just makes my stomach tie up in knots, so I have to keep a tight rein on things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin?\n\nPatient: Too thin? Well, I don't know about too thin. I look in the mirror and... well, it's about standards, really. If my belt feels a notch looser, I feel like I've done right by myself. It's a good feeling. But if my stomach looks the least bit rounded in the mirror, I just feel sloppy and disappointed. It's like letting yourself go. \n\nSpeaking of belts, I've had this one leather belt for... oh, it has to be thirty years now. Genuine cowhide, made in Texas. You just can't find leather like that anymore. Everything now is that bonded stuff that peels apart after a year. I used to polish it every month with saddle soap, the same tin of saddle soap I bought when the kids were still in grade school. The tin has a little dent on the side from when my youngest, Tommy, knocked it off the porch railing trying to catch a firefly. We used to have jars and jars of fireflies on the porch in the summer. You don't see as many fireflies these days, do you? I read somewhere it's because of all the streetlights confusing them. Or maybe it's the pesticides. We never used pesticides on our lawn, just let the clover grow. Clover is good for the bees, you know. But anyway, no, I don't think I look too thin. I just don't want to look sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about how your body looks if you gain weight?\n\nPatient: Worry about how it looks? Well, I don't know about that. I'm not exactly trying to be a movie star at my age. I don't stand there fretting over my reflection, if that's what you mean. \n\nSpeaking of movie stars, though, did you ever go to the old Majestic Theater downtown before they tore it down? Oh, it was a beautiful building. They had real brass fixtures out in the lobby and these heavy, dark red velvet curtains that would pull back right before the picture started. My wife and I, our very first date was at the Majestic. We saw a double feature, must have been 1964 or '65. Popcorn was practically pennies back then. Now you go to a theater and you have to take out a second mortgage just to get a soda. And the floors are always sticky now, have you noticed that? \n\nAt the Majestic, they had an usher with a little flashlight who would actually sweep up between the showings. A young fellow named Arthur, he lived just down the street from my parents. He kept that place spotless. Joined the Navy later on, I believe, and moved out to California. But anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I don't really sit around worrying about my figure or anything like that. I just like to keep things exactly the way they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight affects how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's exactly what brings me in here today. My wife is convinced I'm not eating enough, always fussing over my plate and watching me. But really, most days I just have a cup of coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and then maybe some soup or a few vegetables later on. Big meals just feel completely unnecessary at my age. You just don't need all that heavy food when you're getting on in years. \n\nAnd speaking of coffee, I still make mine in one of those old stovetop percolators. You know the ones, with the little glass knob on the lid? You just watch the dark water bubble up into the glass. My mother had one exactly like it when I was a boy. She kept it on the back burner from dawn till dusk. We used to get our coffee beans from a little grocer down on 4th street, a fellow named Mr. Henderson. He had this massive cast-iron grinder right there on the checkout counter. It made a terrible racket, just a deafening grinding noise, but oh, the smell was incredible. \n\nMr. Henderson had a glass eye, you know. Lost his real one in the First World War, over in France somewhere. He used to tell us kids he could see right through the glass one if he squinted hard enough, and we believed him! We really did. Kids will believe just about anything you tell them, won't they? We used to stand there staring at his eye while he ground the coffee, wondering what he was looking at. \n\nBut... oh, what did you ask me? I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Right, about my eating. Yes, just coffee and toast for me, maybe some soup. Like I said, my wife worries, but I just don't see the need for big meals anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat less because you want to maintain a certain shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I did just mention that a minute ago, didn't I? It's like I was saying, if my belt feels a notch looser, I feel like I've done right by myself. It's a good feeling, you know? But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit rounded, I just feel sloppy. Disappointed in myself. I don't want to look like I've let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of letting things go, you should see the state of the old community center down the road from us. It's an absolute shame. The paint is peeling off in sheets, and half the windows are boarded up. We used to have our town hall meetings there, and the local rotary club would host a pancake breakfast every spring. My wife and I would volunteer to flip the pancakes. We had these huge griddles, must have been three feet across. \n\nOne year, the mayor—Mayor Wilkins, this was back in the late seventies—he tried to flip a pancake and it landed right on his shoe! A brand new pair of wingtips, ruined with syrup. He was a good sport about it, though. Laughed it off. You don't see politicians like that anymore, do you? Now they're all so stiff and worried about their image on the television. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Right, my appearance. Yes, I just don't want to look sloppy. That's why I like my belt to feel a bit loose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating more would make you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Well, yes, I suppose I do. When my wife tries to put butter on things or, you know, serves me a full plate of dinner, I get nervous. I just worry that I'll start putting the weight back on and lose the control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of butter, did you know they used to deliver it right to your door? Oh yes, milk and butter, right in a little insulated box on the porch. The milkman we had when we first bought our house, his name was Charlie. He drove one of those funny little trucks where you stand up to drive. He always left a little treat for our dog, Buster. Buster was a golden retriever mix, sweetest dog you'd ever meet, but he had a terrible habit of chasing the mailman. Never the milkman, though! Charlie had him bought and paid for with those dog biscuits. \n\nCharlie's route got bought out by one of the big dairies eventually, and they stopped doing home delivery altogether. It's a shame how everything just gets swallowed up by the big corporations nowadays, isn't it? You can't even find a decent local hardware store anymore, just those massive warehouses where nobody knows your name and you can never find anyone to help you find a simple wood screw. \n\nBut... oh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I apologize. What was the question? Ah, yes, the anxiety. Yes, the full plates and the butter, it just makes me nervous about losing my control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to maintain a certain weight is more about how you feel or how you look?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's a bit of both, really. Like I was saying before, it's about that feeling of doing right by myself when my belt is a notch looser. But it's also how I look in the mirror—if I see even the slightest curve to my stomach, I just feel so sloppy. It's disappointing. \n\nYou know, speaking of mirrors, we used to have this magnificent antique mirror in our front hallway. It had this heavy, carved wooden frame, mahogany, I think. My wife's grandfather brought it over from Ireland when he immigrated. They came over on a boat, took weeks to cross the Atlantic. Can you imagine? Being stuck on a ship that long, with just a trunk of belongings? \n\nHe settled in Boston first, worked on the docks for a while. It was tough work, hauling crates all day in the freezing cold. He used to tell stories about how the harbor would freeze over in the winter, and they'd have to break the ice just to get the ships in. People were tougher back then, I think. They had to be. Nowadays, people complain if the Wi-Fi goes out for five minutes. \n\nMy grandson, he's always on that phone of his. I ask him what he's looking at, and he says he's talking to his friends. But they're not even talking! Just typing with their thumbs. It's a strange world we live in now. \n\nBut... oh, goodness, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry, doctor. What were we discussing? Ah, the weight and how I look. Yes, it's just that I don't want to look sloppy. That loose belt just makes me feel better about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more driven by a fear of gaining weight or by how you want to look?\n\nPatient: Well, I think it's... it's really the fear, more than anything. Like I mentioned before, when my wife tries to slip a little butter onto my vegetables or sets down a plate that's just too full, my chest gets tight. I just get so nervous that I'll start putting the weight back on and lose all that control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of vegetables, we used to have the most marvelous garden in the backyard. Tomatoes, cucumbers, zucchini—oh, the zucchini! It would grow so fast you could almost watch it get bigger. My wife used to make this wonderful zucchini bread. She’d bake loaves and loaves of it and give it away to the neighbors. \n\nOur next-door neighbor back then was Mr. Henderson. He was a retired train conductor. Used to wear his old cap when he mowed the lawn. He had this incredible model train set in his basement. Took up the whole room! He built little mountains out of papier-mâché and painted tiny little trees. He even had a little diner that lit up from the inside. \n\nHe invited my grandson over to see it once when he was just a little tyke. The boy was absolutely mesmerized. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now kids just want to play those video games where everything is on a screen. It's a shame, really. Working with your hands, building something from scratch... it builds character. \n\nBut... oh, my, I've gone off the rails again, haven't I? Just like Mr. Henderson's trains! I'm so sorry, doctor. What were you asking me? Oh, right, the fear of gaining weight. Yes, it's definitely the fear. Losing that control over my weight... it just makes me terribly nervous.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the fear plays a part, but honestly, what really brought me in today is that my wife thinks I'm not eating enough. I try to tell her, most days I just have my coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and that's plenty. \n\nSpeaking of coffee, we used to have this old aluminum percolator that we kept on the stove. Made the absolute best coffee, strong enough to wake the dead. My father used to drink it black as pitch. He worked at the old auto plant, you see. Thirty-two years on the assembly line, putting on car doors. He’d be up at four in the morning every single day, never even needed an alarm clock. He just had this beautiful silver pocket watch. I still have it, actually, sitting in my top dresser drawer. You have to wind it by hand every day or it stops ticking. They really built things to last back then, didn't they? Now you buy a toaster and the heating element burns out in six months. It’s all just cheap plastic and wires. \n\nBut... oh, listen to me rambling on about toasters. I apologize, doctor. What were we saying? Oh, right, my meals. Yes, so just the coffee and toast, and then maybe some soup or a few vegetables later in the day. At my age, big meals just feel completely unnecessary, you know?\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to maintain a certain appearance?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it really comes down to the fear of putting the weight back on. I mean, I think I mentioned this just a bit ago, but if my wife tries to slip a little butter onto my vegetables, or if she sets down a plate in front of me that just looks too full... my chest gets tight. I just get so nervous that I'll start gaining it all back and lose all that control I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of control, you know, my father used to drive this old Ford pickup truck. Manual transmission, column shift—three on the tree, they called it. You really had to know how to handle a clutch back then. He taught me how to drive it out in the empty parking lot behind the old textile mill on Sundays. That mill employed half the town back in the fifties. They made these thick woolen blankets. Every house had one on the bed in the winter. Heavy things, weighed about ten pounds, but they kept you warm even when the furnace couldn't keep up. \n\nThe mill shut down, oh, must have been in the late seventies. Sent all the jobs overseas. It was a real blow to the community. People had to pack up and move away just to find work. The old building sat empty for decades, just crumbling away. Kids used to sneak in there and break the windows. Just a few years ago, some developer bought the land and knocked the whole thing down to build luxury condominiums. Can you imagine? Condominiums in our little town. They painted them all these drab gray colors. No character at all. \n\nBut... oh, goodness, I'm wandering off again, aren't I? I get to talking about the old days and I just lose my train of thought. I'm sorry, doctor. You were asking about why I limit my food. Yes, it's the fear of gaining the weight back. I just can't stand the thought of losing that control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose I do think about it, yes. It's more about how things fit and look. If my belt feels a notch looser, well, I feel like I've done right by myself. But if my stomach looks the least bit rounded in the mirror, I just feel sloppy and disappointed. 

Speaking of belts, I used to have this beautiful tooled leather belt. My brother bought it for me at a leather shop in New Mexico back in... oh, it must have been 1968. We took a road trip out West in his old Chevrolet Impala. The one with the big tail fins, you know? We drove all along Route 66. Stopped at the Grand Canyon, stood right on the edge. The colors in the rock as the sun went down... you just can't capture that in a photograph. 

We stayed at these little motor lodges along the way. The kind with the neon signs out front that buzzed all night long. One place in Arizona had a swimming pool shaped like a cactus. Can you believe it? A cactus! We met a fellow there who claimed he used to play guitar with Johnny Cash before he got famous. Played us a few tunes right there by the pool. He wasn't half bad, either. Had this old Martin acoustic guitar that looked like it had been through a war. 

You just don't get road trips like that anymore. Now everyone just flies everywhere, rushing through airports, packed into planes like sardines. You miss all the scenery. You miss the whole point of traveling, really. 

But... oh, dear, I've done it again. Just rambling on about old cars and neon signs. I'm so sorry, doctor. What were we talking about? Oh, right, my stomach in the mirror. Yes, if it looks rounded at all, I just feel terribly sloppy. It bothers me quite a bit.
